The verify step reported no NaN/Inf and no format errors.\n\n## 9-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Reading and decoding the 88 NWB files with `BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_nwb_path` (~300 MB each), which materialises the event traces and the stimulus/trials/eye/running tables, is by far the dominant cost and is I/O plus deserialisation bound. It is amortised with a process pool (22 workers at run time): the full conversion took 31 s wall clock versus 5 m 37 s of user CPU time. The second cost is writing the 4.3 GB pickle (dense float32 neural arrays, 21,624 trials × up to 666 neurons × ~263 frames), plus the memory traffic of copying every trial slice twice (`.copy()` in the worker, then `.astype(np.float32)` at assembly).\n\nii.\n```python\n    with Pool(args.workers) as pool:\n        results = pool.map(process_experiment, [(e, args.signal) for e in eids])\n```\n```python\n    with open(args.out, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f, protocol=4)\n```\n\niii. The AI measured load time per experiment during exploration, checked the machine (128 CPUs, 1 TB RAM) and chose a pool size accordingly; no further optimisation was pursued because the whole conversion finishes in about half a minute.\n\n## 9-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Three Python-level loops remain: (1) the per-flash loop that paints image identity and the change flag into the session-length arrays — this could be done with index arithmetic (`np.repeat` over run lengths, or `np.add.at`/cumulative-sum tricks) since the flash intervals are disjoint and sorted; (2) the per-trial loop over `gc.iterrows()`, which is the slowest way to walk a DataFrame — the `searchsorted` calls for all trial start/stop times are already vectorisable in one call, and `itertuples` or column arrays would avoid building a Series per row; (3) the assembly loop over trials in `main`, where the five output rows are written one trial at a time. Also `image_names.index(n)` inside a list comprehension is a linear scan per flash where a dict lookup would be O(1), and `np.stack([np.asarray(x) for x in ev_table[signal].values])` builds the neural matrix row by row.\n\nii.\n```python\n    for a, b, i, c in zip(starts, stops, idx, is_change):\n        image_id[a:b] = i\n        if c:\n            change[a:b] = 1\n```\n```python\n    for tid, row in gc.iterrows():\n        a = int(np.searchsorted(ts, row.start_time, side='left'))\n        b = int(np.searchsorted(ts, row.stop_time, side='left'))\n```\n```python\n    idx = np.array([image_names.index(n) + 1 for n in flashes.image_name.values])\n```\n\niii. Not discussed in the trajectory; the AI's implicit position is that data loading dominates (31 s total), so these loops were left in their readable form.\n\n## 9-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. (1) Every trial's arrays are copied twice — `.copy()` when the trial is cut in the worker, then `.astype(np.float32)` at assembly even though the array is already float32 (a second full copy of the 4 GB of neural data). (2) The running and pupil traces are concatenated across trials once to compute percentile edges and then re-read trial by trial to apply them. (3) In `--binning global` mode the dict comprehension re-evaluates `np.percentile(run_all, qs)` and `np.percentile(pup_all, qs)` over the full pooled arrays once per session (87 identical computations) instead of once. (4) Image names are coded twice: local per-session codes first, then remapped to global codes through `img_map`. (5) Two `np.searchsorted` passes over the pupil timestamps to get the forward and backward nearest-sample gaps.\n\nii.\n```python\n        edges = {r['eid']: (np.percentile(run_all, qs), np.percentile(pup_all, qs))\n                 for r in results}\n```\n```python\n            neural_s.append(t['neural'].astype(np.float32))\n```\n```python\n        img_map = np.array([0] + [image_names.index(n) + 1\n                                  for n in r['image_names']])\n```\n\niii. Not discussed. The two-stage image coding follows from processing sessions independently in worker processes before the global label set is known; the duplicate `astype` is defensive.\n\n## 9-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. Several items never reach the decoder: (1) `change_time`, `start_time`, `stop_time` and `trial_id` are stored on every trial dict and never used afterwards; (2) `cell_specimen_ids` for all 14,437 cells are written into `metadata['session_info']`, as are per-session bin edges and frame rates — informative, but unused by the decoder; (3) the continuous running and pupil traces are carried per trial through the whole pipeline and discarded once the quintile labels are computed; (4) image identity, change, running and pupil are computed for *all* frames of the session, including the 5-minute gray-screen blocks, the natural-movie block and the inter-trial gaps, although only the frames inside go/catch trials are kept; (5) per-cell SD normalisation is computed over the full session for the same reason; (6) the `pupil_valid` gap arrays are computed for every frame but used only as a per-trial average; (7) `exp.events` loads both the `events` and `filtered_events` columns while only one is used; (8) the `input` entries are genuine `(0, T)` empty arrays created per trial, as the task specifies no inputs. Finally, the neural arrays are ~98% zeros but are stored densely, which is what makes the output file 4.3 GB.\n\nii.\n```python\n            outcome=OUTCOMES.index(oc[0]),\n            trial_id=int(tid),\n            change_time=float(row.change_time),\n            start_time=float(row.start_time),\n            stop_time=float(row.stop_time),\n```\n```python\n            cell_specimen_ids=[int(c) for c in r['cell_ids']]))\n```\n```python\n            input_s.append(np.zeros((0, T), dtype=np.float32))\n```\n\niii. The 50% coverage rule was chosen so that a trial's pupil quintiles are based mostly on real measurements rather than long interpolations across blinks; the AI verified that this costs only 0.6% of trials. The ≥2-trial rule follows the explicit format requirement (\"at least two trials within each session\").\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. `exp.events`, i.e. the AllenSDK detected-calcium-event table, using the `filtered_events` column by default (`--signal` can select the raw `events` column instead). Docstring: \"Neural data: detected calcium events (the paper: 'For all analysis of neural data we used the detected calcium events'). We use the AllenSDK 'filtered_events' trace, which is exactly those detected events convolved with a causal half-normal kernel: the raw event trace is nonzero in only ~0.3% of the 32 ms frames, which carries almost no information in a single time bin, while the filtered trace keeps the event times but spreads each event over the following few frames.\" The AI also ran both variants through the reference decoder on an 8-session pilot: filtered_events beat raw events on every output (image identity 0.263 vs 0.202, change 0.636 vs 0.605, running 0.243 vs 0.220).\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Only one processing step beyond reading the trace: each cell is divided by the standard deviation of its own trace over the whole session (zero-SD cells divided by 1). No detrending, smoothing, mean subtraction, or rebinning. Traces are stored as float32 and sliced per trial.\n\nii.\n```python\n    # Scale each cell by the standard deviation of its trace over the whole session.\n    # Event magnitudes are in units of dF/F and vary by orders of magnitude between\n    # cells (and between cre lines / imaging depths); without this, the per-session\n    # projection of the decoder is dominated by a few high-variance cells.\n    sd = neural.std(axis=1, keepdims=True)\n    sd[sd == 0] = 1.0\n    neural = neural / sd\n```\n\niii. Docstring as quoted above. In the trajectory the AI first read the decoder code, noted that the per-session projection is SVD/PCA-initialised on the raw traces with a fixed learning rate so trace scale matters, and then measured the effect on the pilot: per-neuron SD scaling raised validation balanced accuracy for every output (image identity 0.284 vs 0.263, change 0.649 vs 0.636, outcome 0.290 vs 0.255). It describes the step as \"standard, well-justified … prevents high-variance cells dominating the per-session PCA/projection\".\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No neural quality control is applied by the AI. Every cell in `exp.events` (i.e. every ROI that passed the Allen segmentation/QC pipeline) is kept: 14,437 neurons over 87 sessions, 7–666 per session. The AI noticed that the sparse event traces make some trials entirely zero in the smallest sessions and deliberately kept them.\n\nii. No filtering code; the full event table is used:\n```python\n    ev_table = exp.events\n    cell_ids = list(ev_table.index.values)\n```\n```python\n        data['brain_region_idx'].append(\n            np.full(len(r['cell_ids']), regions.index(r['structure']), dtype=np.int64))\n```\n\niii. Trajectory (step 45): \"Warnings are about trials with all-zero neural data, which occur in sessions with very few cells (7-13 Sst/Vip neurons) where the sparse detected events can be entirely absent for an 8-second trial. These are genuine data, not errors.\" The AI also confirmed earlier that `cell_specimen_table.valid_roi` is all True in the released NWBs, i.e. the SDK has already removed invalid ROIs.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Everything is placed on the ophys frame clock (`exp.ophys_timestamps`), as the instructions require (\"temporally align based on ophys timestamp\"). Since the event traces are natively sampled at those frame times, no resampling of the neural data is needed. Each trial is the contiguous slice of frames between the frame index of `trials.start_time` and that of `trials.stop_time` (`side='left'` searchsorted, i.e. the first frame at or after each boundary). `metadata['temporal_alignment_event']` records trial start, with `off_start = 0.0` and `off_end = None` (variable-length trials).\n\nii.\n```python\n    ts = np.asarray(exp.ophys_timestamps, dtype=np.float64)\n    nframes = len(ts)\n    ...\n        a = int(np.searchsorted(ts, row.start_time, side='left'))\n        b = int(np.searchsorted(ts, row.stop_time, side='left'))\n        ...\n            neural=neural[:, a:b].copy(),\n```\n```python\n        temporal_alignment_event=(\n            'trial start (start_time of the go/catch trial in the Allen trials '\n            'table); all data streams are resampled onto the ophys frame '\n            'timestamps of the imaging plane'),\n        off_start=0.0,\n        off_end=None,\n```\n\niii. Docstring: \"All data streams are put on one common clock: the ophys frame timestamps of the imaging plane … The stimulus table, running speed (60 Hz) and pupil (30 Hz eye-tracking camera) are resampled onto those frame times, so one time bin = one ophys frame (~32.3 ms).\" The AI spot-checked one converted trial against the raw session and confirmed that the change flag lands exactly on the frame given by `searchsorted(change_time)` and that flashes occupy ~8 frames (250 ms at 31 Hz).\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. One time bin = one ophys frame. No rebinning, downsampling, or smoothing is applied to any stream; the other streams are interpolated *onto* the frame times instead. The reported `time_bin_size` is the mean over sessions of the median inter-frame interval: 32.3194 ms (~31 Hz). Uniformity across sessions is guaranteed by restricting to the single-plane `VisualBehavior` rig.\n\nii.\n```python\n        dt=float(np.median(np.diff(ts))),\n...\n    dt = float(np.mean([r['dt'] for r in results]))\n    data['metadata'] = dict(..., time_bin_size=dt * 1000.0, ...)\n```\n```python\n  * Only the single-plane VisualBehavior project code. All those experiments are\n    imaged at ~31 Hz, so that every session shares the same time-bin size (as\n    required by the target format). The few Multiscope (11 Hz) sessions available\n    locally (4 sessions from a single mouse) would break that requirement.\n```\n\niii. Docstring as quoted. The AI verified across all 88 candidate sessions that the single-plane rig gives a uniform `dt = 0.03232 s`, whereas Multiscope gives ~11 Hz, and used that as the deciding argument for excluding Multiscope (the paper's own rig) in favour of the format requirement that \"time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions\".\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n\ni. From `exp.stimulus_presentations`, restricted to `active == True` (the change-detection block, excluding the 5-min gray-screen blocks and the natural-movie block) and `omitted == False`: the columns `image_name`, `start_time`, `end_time`. It is **not** derived from the trials table's `initial_image_name` / `change_image_name`. This gives a genuinely per-frame variable: the image code during each 250 ms flash and a dedicated `gray_screen` category (0) during the 500 ms inter-flash gray periods and during omitted flashes.\n\nii.\n```python\n    sp = exp.stimulus_presentations\n    flashes = sp[(sp.active == True) & (sp.omitted == False)]  # noqa: E712\n    image_names = sorted(flashes.image_name.unique())\n    image_id = np.zeros(nframes, dtype=np.int64)   # 0 == gray screen / omitted\n    ...\n    starts = np.searchsorted(ts, flashes.start_time.values, side='left')\n    stops = np.searchsorted(ts, flashes.end_time.values, side='left')\n    idx = np.array([image_names.index(n) + 1 for n in flashes.image_name.values])\n```\n\niii. The instruction asks for \"image identity (of the image presented during the non-grey screen)\"; the AI reads this as the identity of the image actually on the screen at each moment, with gray as its own level, and takes it from the stimulus table, which is the only source that has per-flash times. Its metadata spells out the stimulus structure (\"250 ms flashes separated by 500 ms of gray screen, 5% of flashes omitted\"). Omitted flashes are treated as gray because no image was presented.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image identity*?\n\ni. Two-stage integer coding. Per session, the session's own sorted image names are coded 1..n (0 reserved for gray). At assembly time a global sorted list of image names across all sessions is built and the per-session codes are remapped into the global codes with a small lookup array. All sessions turn out to use the same 8-image familiar set A, so the global list is `['im061','im062','im063','im065','im066','im069','im077','im085']` and `output_values[0] = ['gray_screen', ...those 8]`. In the converted data, gray occupies 66.9% of time bins and each image ~4.1%.\n\nii.\n```python\n    image_names = sorted({n for r in results for n in r['image_names']})\n...\n        img_map = np.array([0] + [image_names.index(n) + 1\n                                  for n in r['image_names']])\n...\n            out[0] = img_map[t['image_id']]\n```\n```python\n                output_values=[\n                    ['gray_screen'] + image_names, ...\n```\n\niii. The AI verified in its scan that every familiar+active session uses image set A with the same 8 images, so a single global code list is consistent across sessions; the mapping is recorded in `output_values` so the labels are recoverable.\n\n## 3-c. How is `output` *Image identity* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The image-code time series is built for the whole session on the ophys frame grid (`searchsorted` of each flash's `start_time`/`end_time`), and then sliced with exactly the same `[a:b]` frame indices as the neural data, so the two are aligned frame-by-frame by construction. A 250 ms flash occupies ~8 frames.\n\nii.\n```python\n    for a, b, i, c in zip(starts, stops, idx, is_change):\n        image_id[a:b] = i\n        if c:\n            change[a:b] = 1\n...\n        trials.append(dict(\n            neural=neural[:, a:b].copy(),\n            image_id=image_id[a:b].copy(), ...))\n```\n\niii. Same one-clock argument as 2-d. The AI's spot-check against the raw session confirmed that the converted image-identity codes match the flash times and the change image for the trial.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image change* derived from?\n\ni. From the same `stimulus_presentations` table, using the `is_change` flag of each active, non-omitted flash (together with that flash's `start_time`/`end_time`). `is_change` is True only for a flash whose image differs from the preceding one, so catch (sham-change) trials never get a 1.\n\nii.\n```python\n    is_change = flashes.is_change.values.astype(bool)\n    for a, b, i, c in zip(starts, stops, idx, is_change):\n        image_id[a:b] = i\n        if c:\n            change[a:b] = 1\n```\n\niii. The instruction says the variable should \"have value of 1 right after a change in image identity, otherwise 0\". Using the stimulus table's own `is_change` flag makes the change indicator consistent with the image-identity trace derived from the same table (the 1 marks exactly the flash at which the identity trace changes value).\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image change*?\n\ni. None beyond the binary indicator: `change` is 1 for the frames of the change flash itself (~8 frames ≈ 250 ms) and 0 everywhere else, including the gray period after the change flash. Across the dataset 2.6% of time bins are labelled `change`. `output_values[1] = ['no_change','change']`.\n\nii. See 4-a; at assembly:\n```python\n            out[1] = t['change']\n```\n\niii. The AI's description is that the flag marks \"whether an image change just occurred\"; it is tied to the duration of the changed image presentation rather than to the full 750 ms image-presentation interval used in the paper's behavioural analysis. The AI verified in its spot-check that \"the change flag lands exactly on the change flash (frame 117 = searchsorted of change_time), flashes last ~8 frames (250 ms at 31 Hz)\".\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Image change* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. No thresholding is needed — the variable is natively binary (0/1, int64), produced directly from the `is_change` boolean.\n\nii.\n```python\n        if c:\n            change[a:b] = 1\n```\n```python\n                output_values=[..., ['no_change', 'change'], ...]\n```\n\niii. N/A — a binary flag as specified by the instructions.\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Image change* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Identically to image identity: built on the full-session ophys frame grid from flash start/end times, then sliced with the same trial frame indices as the neural data.\n\nii.\n```python\n            change=change[a:b].copy(),\n```\n\niii. Same single-clock argument as 2-d/3-c; verified in the spot-check that the change frame index equals `searchsorted(ts, change_time)`.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Running speed* derived from?\n\ni. `exp.running_speed`, using its `timestamps` and `speed` columns (the running-disc encoder signal, ~60 Hz, in cm/s).\n\nii.\n```python\n    rs = exp.running_speed\n    running = np.interp(ts, rs.timestamps.values, rs.speed.values)\n```\n\niii. The SDK's standard locomotion interface; the AI checked its sampling rate (dt ≈ 0.0167 s) and time coverage against the ophys timestamps during exploration.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. Linear interpolation onto the ophys frame timestamps (`np.interp`, which clamps to the first/last sample outside the encoder's time range rather than producing NaN), no smoothing or filtering, followed by quintile discretization (see 5-c). The continuous interpolated values are carried per trial and only converted to bins at assembly time.\n\nii.\n```python\n    running = np.interp(ts, rs.timestamps.values, rs.speed.values)\n...\n            running=running[a:b].copy(),\n...\n            out[2] = np.searchsorted(run_edges, t['running'], side='right')\n```\n\niii. Docstring: \"The stimulus table, running speed (60 Hz) and pupil (30 Hz eye-tracking camera) are resampled onto those frame times, so one time bin = one ophys frame (~32.3 ms).\" The SDK already hardware-syncs all streams to a common clock, so interpolation onto the ophys timestamps is the only step needed.\n\n## 5-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Five equal-occupancy (quintile) bins, with the bin edges computed **per session** from all retained time bins of that session (an option `--binning global` computes one set of edges pooled across all sessions, but the default and the shipped dataset use per-session edges). Bins are assigned with `searchsorted(edges, x, side='right')`, giving labels 0–4; the edges are recorded per session in `metadata['session_info']`. The resulting marginal distribution is exactly 20% per bin.\n\nii.\n```python\n    qs = np.linspace(0, 100, NBINS + 1)[1:-1]\n    if args.binning == 'global':\n        run_all = np.concatenate([t['running'] for r in results for t in r['trials']])\n        ...\n    else:\n        edges = {}\n        for r in results:\n            run_all = np.concatenate([t['running'] for t in r['trials']])\n            pup_all = np.concatenate([t['pupil'] for t in r['trials']])\n            edges[r['eid']] = (np.percentile(run_all, qs), np.percentile(pup_all, qs))\n```\n```python\n            out[2] = np.searchsorted(run_edges, t['running'], side='right')\n```\n\niii. Code comment: \"Pupil size is measured in camera pixels and its absolute value differs by up to ~3x between sessions/mice (eye size, camera position), and mice also differ strongly in how much they run. The decoder has a separate projection per session, so only within-session variation can be decoded. Bin edges are therefore computed per session (equal-occupancy quintiles within a session).\" In the pilot the AI observed that globally pooled edges left some sessions with 81% of samples in a single bin, i.e. an almost constant target within those sessions, and switched to per-session quintiles for that reason.\n\n## 5-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The interpolation target is the ophys timestamp vector itself, so the running series is already on the neural time grid for the whole session; the trial slice uses the same `[a:b]` indices as the neural data.\n\nii.\n```python\n    ts = np.asarray(exp.ophys_timestamps, dtype=np.float64)\n    running = np.interp(ts, rs.timestamps.values, rs.speed.values)\n...\n            neural=neural[:, a:b].copy(),\n            running=running[a:b].copy(),\n```\n\niii. Same argument as 2-d: one common clock for all streams, so the alignment is guaranteed by construction.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Pupil diameter* derived from?\n\ni. `exp.eye_tracking`, using `pupil_area`, `likely_blink` and `timestamps` (30 Hz eye camera). The diameter is computed from the area rather than taken from `pupil_width`; frames flagged `likely_blink` are set to NaN before interpolation. Sessions with an empty eye-tracking table or fewer than 100 finite samples are dropped.\n\nii.\n```python\n    et = exp.eye_tracking\n    if len(et) == 0:\n        return dict(eid=eid, skip='no eye tracking')\n    area = et.pupil_area.values.astype(np.float64).copy()\n    area[et.likely_blink.values.astype(bool)] = np.nan  # blinks are not measurements\n    diam = 2.0 * np.sqrt(area / np.pi)\n    good = np.isfinite(diam)\n    if good.sum() < 100:\n        return dict(eid=eid, skip='no valid pupil samples')\n```\n\niii. Metadata: `pupil_measure='diameter = 2*sqrt(pupil_area/pi) in camera pixels, blinks removed and interpolated'` — i.e. the equivalent-circle diameter of the fitted pupil ellipse, which uses both ellipse axes instead of one. The AI measured the blink fraction (~2–4% of frames, with occasional long gaps) across all candidate sessions before deciding how to handle them.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Pupil diameter*?\n\ni. Blink frames removed → linear interpolation of the remaining samples onto the ophys frame times (`np.interp`, so blink gaps are bridged) → per-frame validity flag marking frames whose nearest *real* sample is more than 0.5 s away → quintile discretization (6-c). The validity flag is used only to reject trials (>50% invalid), not to alter the values.\n\nii.\n```python\n    et_ts = et.timestamps.values.astype(np.float64)\n    # interpolate the (blink) gaps, on the eye-tracking clock, then onto ophys frames\n    pupil = np.interp(ts, et_ts[good], diam[good])\n    # frames whose nearest valid pupil sample is far away are flagged as missing\n    nearest = np.abs(ts - et_ts[good][np.clip(\n        np.searchsorted(et_ts[good], ts), 0, good.sum() - 1)])\n    nearest_prev = np.abs(ts - et_ts[good][np.clip(\n        np.searchsorted(et_ts[good], ts) - 1, 0, good.sum() - 1)])\n    gap = np.minimum(nearest, nearest_prev)\n    pupil_valid = gap < 0.5  # within half a second of a real measurement\n```\n\niii. Interpolating across short blinks keeps the trial intact, while the 0.5 s gap criterion prevents long stretches of invented values from being fed to the decoder as if they were measurements; trials that are mostly invented are dropped instead (see 1-e). The AI chose the 50%/0.5 s thresholds after quantifying blink fractions and maximum NaN gaps per session.\n\n## 6-c. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Exactly as running speed: five equal-occupancy quintile bins with per-session edges (default) computed from all retained frames of that session, assigned with `searchsorted(..., side='right')` to labels 0–4, edges stored per session in `session_info`. The marginal distribution is 20% per bin.\n\nii.\n```python\n            edges[r['eid']] = (np.percentile(run_all, qs), np.percentile(pup_all, qs))\n...\n            out[3] = np.searchsorted(pup_edges, t['pupil'], side='right')\n```\n\niii. The per-session rationale is stated primarily for pupil: absolute pupil size in camera pixels differs up to ~3× across sessions/mice because of eye size and camera placement, so globally pooled quintiles mostly encode which session you are in, which the per-session projection of the decoder cannot exploit; within-session quintiles encode arousal fluctuations, which it can.\n\n## 6-d. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same as running speed: interpolated onto the session's ophys timestamps and then sliced with the same trial frame indices as the neural data.\n\nii.\n```python\n    pupil = np.interp(ts, et_ts[good], diam[good])\n...\n            pupil=pupil[a:b].copy(),\n            neural=neural[:, a:b].copy(),\n```\n\niii. Single common clock (the ophys frame times), as stated in the docstring; eye-tracking timestamps are already synced to that clock by the SDK.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. The four mutually exclusive boolean columns of the trials table — `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, `correct_reject` — for the selected go/catch trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nOUTCOMES = ['hit', 'miss', 'false_alarm', 'correct_reject']\n...\n        oc = [o for o in OUTCOMES if bool(row[o])]\n        if len(oc) != 1:\n            continue\n        trials.append(dict(..., outcome=OUTCOMES.index(oc[0]), ...))\n```\n\niii. These are the SDK's canonical change-detection outcome labels. The AI verified across all 88 candidate sessions that \"every go/catch trial has exactly one outcome\", and made the \"exactly one\" requirement an explicit guard rather than an assumption.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Trial outcome*?\n\ni. Mapped to a fixed integer code 0–3 in the order hit, miss, false_alarm, correct_reject, then broadcast to a constant time series over the trial's frames so that all five outputs share one `(5, T)` array. Observed distribution: hit 29.9%, miss 57.6%, false alarm 2.1%, correct reject 10.5%.\n\nii.\n```python\n            out[4] = t['outcome']\n...\n                output_values=[..., ['hit', 'miss', 'false_alarm', 'correct_reject']]\n```\n\niii. Trajectory (step 53): \"the task says … trial outcome is 'Static per-trial'; I made outcome a constant time series within the trial, which is acceptable (all outputs in one array must share the time dimension).\"\n\n## 8. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Missing pupil is the main case and is handled at three levels: blink samples are removed (NaN) rather than used; short gaps are interpolated; frames more than 0.5 s from any real sample are flagged invalid; trials with >50% invalid frames are dropped; sessions with no eye tracking or <100 finite samples are dropped whole. Trials shorter than 2 frames, and trials whose outcome flags are ambiguous, are dropped. Running speed outside the encoder's coverage is clamped to the nearest sample by `np.interp` rather than becoming NaN. Neurons with a zero-SD trace are divided by 1 instead of 0. Behaviour-block boundaries are handled by taking only `active`, non-omitted flashes, with everything else labelled gray. All-zero neural trials in very-small-cell-count sessions are knowingly kept as real data. There is no `try/except` around per-experiment processing, so an unexpected read failure in one worker would abort the whole pool rather than skipping that session.\n\nii.\n```python\n    sd = neural.std(axis=1, keepdims=True)\n    sd[sd == 0] = 1.0\n```\n```python\n    if len(et) == 0:\n        return dict(eid=eid, skip='no eye tracking')\n    ...\n    if good.sum() < 100:\n        return dict(eid=eid, skip='no valid pupil samples')\n```\n```python\n        if b - a < 2:\n            continue\n        if np.mean(pupil_valid[a:b]) < 0.5:\n            continue\n        oc = [o for o in OUTCOMES if bool(row[o])]\n        if len(oc) != 1:\n            continue\n```\n```python\n    results = [r for r in results if r.get('skip') is None and len(r['trials']) >= 2]\n```\n\niii. The AI pre-scanned every candidate session for pupil NaN fraction, maximum NaN gap, outcome completeness, NaN change times and window in-bounds before writing the converter, then confirmed afterwards that only one experiment (806456687, zero eye-tracking rows) and 0.6% of trials were lost. The verify step reported no NaN/Inf and no format errors.\n\n## 9-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Reading and decoding the 88 NWB files with `BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_nwb_path` (~300 MB each), which materialises the event traces and the stimulus/trials/eye/running tables, is by far the dominant cost and is I/O plus deserialisation bound. It is amortised with a process pool (22 workers at run time): the full conversion took 31 s wall clock versus 5 m 37 s of user CPU time. The second cost is writing the 4.3 GB pickle (dense float32 neural arrays, 21,624 trials × up to 666 neurons × ~263 frames), plus the memory traffic of copying every trial slice twice (`.copy()` in the worker, then `.astype(np.float32)` at assembly).\n\nii.\n```python\n    with Pool(args.workers) as pool:\n        results = pool.map(process_experiment, [(e, args.signal) for e in eids])\n```\n```python\n    with open(args.out, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f, protocol=4)\n```\n\niii. The AI measured load time per experiment during exploration, checked the machine (128 CPUs, 1 TB RAM) and chose a pool size accordingly; no further optimisation was pursued because the whole conversion finishes in about half a minute.\n\n## 9-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Three Python-level loops remain: (1) the per-flash loop that paints image identity and the change flag into the session-length arrays — this could be done with index arithmetic (`np.repeat` over run lengths, or `np.add.at`/cumulative-sum tricks) since the flash intervals are disjoint and sorted; (2) the per-trial loop over `gc.iterrows()`, which is the slowest way to walk a DataFrame — the `searchsorted` calls for all trial start/stop times are already vectorisable in one call, and `itertuples` or column arrays would avoid building a Series per row; (3) the assembly loop over trials in `main`, where the five output rows are written one trial at a time. Also `image_names.index(n)` inside a list comprehension is a linear scan per flash where a dict lookup would be O(1), and `np.stack([np.asarray(x) for x in ev_table[signal].values])` builds the neural matrix row by row.\n\nii.\n```python\n    for a, b, i, c in zip(starts, stops, idx, is_change):\n        image_id[a:b] = i\n        if c:\n            change[a:b] = 1\n```\n```python\n    for tid, row in gc.iterrows():\n        a = int(np.searchsorted(ts, row.start_time, side='left'))\n        b = int(np.searchsorted(ts, row.stop_time, side='left'))\n```\n```python\n    idx = np.array([image_names.index(n) + 1 for n in flashes.image_name.values])\n```\n\niii. Not discussed in the trajectory; the AI's implicit position is that data loading dominates (31 s total), so these loops were left in their readable form.\n\n## 9-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. (1) Every trial's arrays are copied twice — `.copy()` when the trial is cut in the worker, then `.astype(np.float32)` at assembly even though the array is already float32 (a second full copy of the 4 GB of neural data). (2) The running and pupil traces are concatenated across trials once to compute percentile edges and then re-read trial by trial to apply them. (3) In `--binning global` mode the dict comprehension re-evaluates `np.percentile(run_all, qs)` and `np.percentile(pup_all, qs)` over the full pooled arrays once per session (87 identical computations) instead of once. (4) Image names are coded twice: local per-session codes first, then remapped to global codes through `img_map`. (5) Two `np.searchsorted` passes over the pupil timestamps to get the forward and backward nearest-sample gaps.\n\nii.\n```python\n        edges = {r['eid']: (np.percentile(run_all, qs), np.percentile(pup_all, qs))\n                 for r in results}\n```\n```python\n            neural_s.append(t['neural'].astype(np.float32))\n```\n```python\n        img_map = np.array([0] + [image_names.index(n) + 1\n                                  for n in r['image_names']])\n```\n\niii. Not discussed. The two-stage image coding follows from processing sessions independently in worker processes before the global label set is known; the duplicate `astype` is defensive.\n\n## 9-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. Several items never reach the decoder: (1) `change_time`, `start_time`, `stop_time` and `trial_id` are stored on every trial dict and never used afterwards; (2) `cell_specimen_ids` for all 14,437 cells are written into `metadata['session_info']`, as are per-session bin edges and frame rates — informative, but unused by the decoder; (3) the continuous running and pupil traces are carried per trial through the whole pipeline and discarded once the quintile labels are computed; (4) image identity, change, running and pupil are computed for *all* frames of the session, including the 5-minute gray-screen blocks, the natural-movie block and the inter-trial gaps, although only the frames inside go/catch trials are kept; (5) per-cell SD normalisation is computed over the full session for the same reason; (6) the `pupil_valid` gap arrays are computed for every frame but used only as a per-trial average; (7) `exp.events` loads both the `events` and `filtered_events` columns while only one is used; (8) the `input` entries are genuine `(0, T)` empty arrays created per trial, as the task specifies no inputs. Finally, the neural arrays are ~98% zeros but are stored densely, which is what makes the output file 4.3 GB.\n\nii.\n```python\n            outcome=OUTCOMES.index(oc[0]),\n            trial_id=int(tid),\n            change_time=float(row.change_time),\n            start_time=float(row.start_time),\n            stop_time=float(row.stop_time),\n```\n```python\n            cell_specimen_ids=[int(c) for c in r['cell_ids']]))\n```\n```python\n            input_s.append(np.zeros((0, T), dtype=np.float32))\n```\n\niii. Not discussed explicitly; the extra per-trial fields and `session_info` entries were kept for provenance and for the AI's own verification passes (it used `session_info` afterwards to check kept-trial fractions and to identify the dropped experiment), and the dense format is imposed by the target data specification.\nMDEOF\necho done; wc -l /logs/verifier/judge/claude/DECISIONS.md","description":"Write DECISIONS.md"}}}

They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Your code must implement the decisions as you describe them.\n# Decisions\n\n## 1-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. Subjects (mice) and sessions are discovered from the Allen SDK's `VisualBehaviorOphysProjectCache`, filtered to the `VisualBehavior` project code. All data are pulled using the `get_ophys_experiment_table()` method. For each mouse, all ophys sessions are loaded via `get_behavior_ophys_experiment()`. Data are loaded using the `allensdk` library's S3 cache.\n\nii. Finding all data:\n```python\nbc = bpc.VisualBehaviorOphysProjectCache.from_s3_cache(cache_dir=cache_dir)\nexperiment_table = bc.get_ophys_experiment_table()\nvb_experiments = experiment_table[experiment_table.project_code == PROJECT_CODE]\nall_mouse_ids = sorted(vb_experiments.mouse_id.unique())\n```\n\nLoading a session's experiments:\n```python\nfor exp_id in session_experiments.index:\n    datasets[exp_id] = bc.get_behavior_ophys_experiment(exp_id)\n```\n\niii. The experiment table is the SDK's canonical listing of all experiments. Filtering by `project_code == 'VisualBehavior'` selects the single-plane ophys experiments. Each experiment corresponds to one imaging plane in one session; sessions are reconsturcted by combining `experiments` are grouped by the same `ophys_session_id`.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. Subjects correspond to unique `mouse_id` values in the experiment table.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_mouse_ids = sorted(vb_experiments.mouse_id.unique())\n```\n\niii. The `mouse_id` field is the SDK's unique identifier for each animal. The number of mice can be cross-checked against the project documentation.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each session corresponds to a unique `ophys_session_id` in the experiment table. A single session may contain multiple experiments (imaging planes), which are grouped together by matching `ophys_session_id`. Sessions for each mouse are sorted by `date_of_acquisition`.\n\nii.\n```python\nmouse_exps = vb_experiments[vb_experiments.mouse_id == mouse_id]\nmouse_sessions = mouse_exps.drop_duplicates(subset='ophys_session_id')[\n    ['ophys_session_id', 'session_type', 'date_of_acquisition']\n].sort_values(by='date_of_acquisition')\nsession_ids = mouse_sessions['ophys_session_id'].values\n...\nsess_exps = mouse_exps[mouse_exps.ophys_session_id == sid]\n```\n\niii. The `ophys_session_id` groups all imaging planes recorded simultaneously in one behavioral session. Sorting by acquisition date preserves the chronological order of sessions within each mouse.\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. Trials are defined using the built-in `dataset.trials` table from the Allen SDK. Each trial corresponds to one stimulus change event (go or catch). The full trial window from `start_time` to `stop_time` is used, giving variable-length trials (typically ~80-90 frames / ~8s). Aborted trials, auto-rewarded trials, and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrials_table = ref_ds.trials\n...\nvalid_trials = trials_table[\n    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n]\n\nfor _, row in valid_trials.iterrows():\n    start_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\n    end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\n    if end_idx > T:\n        end_idx = T\n    if end_idx <= start_idx:\n        continue\n    idx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n```\n\niii. The SDK's built-in trials table was used, since it provides pre-computed trial metadata (outcome, image identity, timing). The full trial window (`start_time` to `stop_time`) is used rather than a fixed window around `change_time`, so that the trial includes both pre-change stimulus flashes and the post-change response window. This enables time-varying output variables (image identity changes mid-trial). Trials are typically ~8s: ~4s pre-change (variable number of stimulus flashes) and ~4.2s post-change (fixed response window).\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Aborted trials (early lick before change), auto-rewarded trials (free reward), and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded. Trials where `end_idx <= start_idx` (empty window) are skipped. If `stop_time` extends past the recording, the trial is clipped to the end. Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded from the final output.\n\nii.\n```python\nvalid_trials = trials_table[\n    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n]\n...\nif end_idx > T:\n    end_idx = T\nif end_idx <= start_idx:\n    continue\n...\nif len(trials) < 2:\n    continue\n```\n\niii. Per instruction, we are ignoring aborted and auto-rewarded trials. Aborted trials were excluded because the mouse licked before the change occurred, so no change stimulus was presented. Auto-rewarded trials were excluded because the free reward biases the behavioral response. Requiring a valid `change_time` ensures there is a well-defined change point within the trial. The minimum 2-trial threshold prevents degenerate sessions from entering the dataset.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from the `dff_traces` (dF/F calcium fluorescence traces) from each experiment in the session, accessed via `dataset.dff_traces.dff`.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\nneural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n```\n\niii. dF/F is the standard measure of neural activity for two-photon calcium imaging. The Allen SDK provides it pre-computed with neuropil correction and baseline normalization already applied.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The only processing is combining neurons from multiple imaging planes within a session by vertically stacking their dF/F arrays. Each neuron is tagged with a plane label (`{area}_{depth}um`) for brain region tracking.\n\nii.\n```python\ndff_list = []\nplane_labels = []\nfor exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n    area = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'targeted_structure']\n    depth = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'imaging_depth']\n    plane_labels.extend([f'{area}_{depth}um'] * len(ds.dff_traces))\nneural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n```\n\niii. The dF/F traces are already processed by the Allen SDK pipeline (motion correction, neuropil subtraction, dF/F normalization). No additional filtering or normalization was applied. Plane labels were constructed to preserve brain region information for downstream analysis.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No additional quality filtering is applied to the neural data. All neurons present in the SDK's `dff_traces` are included.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The Allen SDK pipeline already applies its own quality control (e.g., cell segmentation, neuropil correction). No further filtering was deemed necessary.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Each trial's neural data is aligned to the trial start (`start_time`). The ophys frames from `start_time` to `stop_time` are extracted, giving a variable-length window per trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nstart_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\nend_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\nidx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n...\n'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n```\n\niii. `np.searchsorted` finds the first ophys frame at or after each boundary time. Since the frame rate is ~11 Hz, the maximum alignment error is ~45 ms (half a frame). The full trial window is used so that time-varying output variables (image identity, image change) can capture the pre- and post-change periods.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. The neural data is not binned or resampled. It is kept at the native ophys frame rate (~11 Hz). The time bin size is computed from the median inter-frame interval of `ophys_timestamps`.\n\nii.\n```python\nfirst_ophys_ts = session_results[0][1]['ophys_ts']\ntime_bin_size_ms = float(np.median(np.diff(first_ophys_ts)) * 1000)\n```\n\niii. The ophys timestamps are already at a consistent frame rate determined by the microscope scanning. No resampling is needed since all data (neural, running, pupil) are aligned to the same ophys timebase.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n\ni. Image name is a time-varying variable derived from both `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` columns in the trials table, combined with `change_time` to determine when the image switches.\n\nii.\n```python\nchange_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\nimage_names = np.empty(n_frames, dtype=object)\nimage_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\nimage_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n```\n\niii. Since the trial window now spans both pre- and post-change periods, image identity varies within a trial. Before `change_time`, the initial image is on screen; after, the change image is displayed. For catch trials (sham change), `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` are the same, so the image identity is constant throughout.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image identity*?\n\ni. Image names are mapped to integer codes via a global mapping built from all unique image names across all sessions. The integer code varies within a trial (initial image code before change, change image code after).\n\nii.\n```python\nall_image_names = sorted(all_image_names)\nimage_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(all_image_names)}\n...\nimage_row = np.array(\n    [image_to_code[name] for name in t['image_names']],\n    dtype=np.int8)\n```\n\niii. A global mapping ensures consistent integer codes across sessions. Sorting the image names makes the mapping deterministic. The mapping is stored in `metadata['image_to_code']` so it can be recovered for interpretation.\n\n## 3-c. How is `output` *Image identity* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Image name is computed per ophys frame within the trial window, using the same `idx` array as the neural data. The switch point is determined by `np.searchsorted` on `change_time`.\n\nii.\n```python\nchange_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\nimage_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\nimage_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n```\n\niii. The image identity at each frame is determined by whether that frame falls before or after `change_time`. This is aligned to the neural data because both use the same ophys frame indices.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image change* derived from?\n\ni. Image change is a binary time-varying variable derived from `change_time` and the `go` column in the trials table. It is 1 only during the first image flash and following grey period (750ms window starting at `change_time`), and only for **go trials** where an actual image change occurs. For catch trials (sham change), `image_change` is 0 throughout.\n\nii.\n```python\nimage_change = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=np.int8)\nif row['go']:\n    change_end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'] + 0.75)\n    image_change[change_idx:change_end_idx] = 1\n```\n\niii. The 750ms window corresponds to one stimulus flash (250ms) plus the following grey inter-stimulus interval (500ms). This marks only the transient change event rather than the entire post-change period. Catch trials are excluded because no actual image change occurs — the `go` column distinguishes real changes from sham changes.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image change*?\n\ni. No processing beyond computing the binary indicator from `change_time` and `go` via `np.searchsorted`.\n\nii. See 3-a.\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Image change* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same as image name — computed per ophys frame using the same `idx` array and `change_time` alignment.\n\nii. See 3-a.\n\niii. Same frame-level alignment as image name and neural data.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Running speed* derived from?\n\ni. Running speed is derived from `dataset.running_speed`, which provides speed and timestamps from the running wheel encoder.\n\nii.\n```python\nrun = ref_ds.running_speed\n```\n\niii. The `running_speed` attribute is the SDK's standard interface for locomotion data.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. Running speed is linearly interpolated from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. NaN values (from extrapolation) are mapped to bin 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nf_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\nrunning_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n...\nall_running = np.concatenate([\n    np.concatenate([t['running'] for t in trials])\n    for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n])\nrun_edges = discretize(all_running, N_LEVELS)\n...\nrun_disc = apply_discretize(t['running'], run_edges)\n```\n\niii. Linear interpolation preserves the signal shape while resampling to the ophys timebase. Percentile-based binning ensures roughly equal class counts across bins, which is important for balanced decoding. Bin edges are computed globally across all sessions to maintain consistent categories.\n\n## 5-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Running speed is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data. The same `idx` array is used to extract both.\n\nii.\n```python\nrun = ref_ds.running_speed\nf_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\nrunning_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n\"\"\"Convert Allen Visual Behavior Ophys data to standardized pickle format.\n\nUsage:\n    python -u convert_data.py <output_path.pkl>\n    python -u convert_data.py <output_path.pkl> --sample\n\nData: All mice from the VisualBehavior project (single-plane ophys).\nEach session has one imaging plane. Trials are segmented using the\nbuilt-in trials table (start_time to stop_time, variable length).\n\nOutput variables (decoded from neural activity):\n  - running_speed: percentile-binned across all sessions (time-varying)\n  - pupil_diameter: percentile-binned across all sessions (time-varying)\n  - image_name: categorical image on screen (time-varying, changes at change_time)\n  - image_change: binary, 0 before change_time, 1 at/after (time-varying)\n  - trial_outcome: categorical hit/miss/false_alarm/correct_reject (per-trial)\n\"\"\"\n\nimport sys\nimport time\nimport argparse\nfrom pathlib import Path\nimport pickle\nimport warnings\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\nfrom scipy.interpolate import interp1d\n\n_allen2p_seed = 'x5cidj2hy87s'\nnp.random.seed(sum(ord(c) for c in _allen2p_seed) % 2**31)\n\nwarnings.filterwarnings(\"ignore\", message=\"Ignoring the following cached namespace\")\n\nimport allensdk.brain_observatory.behavior.behavior_project_cache as bpc\n\n# ---------- constants ----------\nN_LEVELS = 5            # discretization levels for running / pupil\nPROJECT_CODE = 'VisualBehavior'\n\nTRIAL_OUTCOMES = ['hit', 'miss', 'false_alarm', 'correct_reject']\n\n\ndef get_cache(cache_dir):\n    return bpc.VisualBehaviorOphysProjectCache.from_s3_cache(cache_dir=cache_dir)\n\n\ndef extract_session_data(bc, session_experiments):\n    \"\"\"Load all planes for one session, merge neural data, resample behavior.\"\"\"\n    datasets = {}\n    for exp_id in session_experiments.index:\n        datasets[exp_id] = bc.get_behavior_ophys_experiment(exp_id)\n\n    ref_ds = list(datasets.values())[0]\n    ophys_ts = ref_ds.ophys_timestamps\n\n    # --- Neural: merge dF/F across planes ---\n    dff_list = []\n    plane_labels = []\n    for exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n        dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n        area = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'targeted_structure']\n        depth = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'imaging_depth']\n        plane_labels.extend([f'{area}_{depth}um'] * len(ds.dff_traces))\n    neural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n\n    # --- Running speed: interpolate to ophys timebase ---\n    run = ref_ds.running_speed\n    f_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n                     kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n    running_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n\n    # --- Pupil diameter: interpolate, exclude blinks ---\n    eye = ref_ds.eye_tracking\n    eye_clean = eye[~eye['likely_blink']]\n    f_pupil = interp1d(eye_clean['timestamps'].values,\n                       eye_clean['pupil_width'].values,\n                       kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n    pupil_diameter = f_pupil(ophys_ts)\n\n    # --- Trials table ---\n    trials_table = ref_ds.trials\n\n    return {\n        'ophys_ts': ophys_ts,\n        'neural_data': neural_data,\n        'plane_labels': plane_labels,\n        'running_speed': running_speed,\n        'pupil_diameter': pupil_diameter,\n        'trials_table': trials_table,\n    }\n\n\ndef segment_trials(session_data):\n    \"\"\"Segment into trials using the built-in trials table.\n\n    For each non-aborted, non-auto-rewarded trial with a valid change_time,\n    extract ophys frames from start_time to stop_time (variable length).\n\n    Returns list of trial dicts with raw (continuous) running/pupil and\n    per-frame image identity and change indicator.\n    \"\"\"\n    ophys_ts = session_data['ophys_ts']\n    neural_data = session_data['neural_data']\n    running_speed = session_data['running_speed']\n    pupil_diameter = session_data['pupil_diameter']\n    trials_table = session_data['trials_table']\n    T = len(ophys_ts)\n\n    # Skip aborted and auto-rewarded trials, and trials without a valid change_time\n    valid_trials = trials_table[\n        (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n        (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n        (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n    ]\n\n    trials = []\n    for _, row in valid_trials.iterrows():\n        start_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\n        end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\n        if end_idx > T:\n            end_idx = T\n        if end_idx <= start_idx:\n            continue\n        idx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n        n_frames = len(idx)\n\n        # Determine trial outcome\n        outcome = 'other'\n        for label in TRIAL_OUTCOMES:\n            if row[label]:\n                outcome = label\n                break\n\n        # Time-varying image name: initial before change, change after\n        change_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\n        image_names = np.empty(n_frames, dtype=object)\n        image_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\n        image_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n\n        # Binary image change indicator: 1 only during first flash + grey (750ms), go trials only\n        image_change = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=np.int8)\n        if row['go']:\n            change_end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'] + 0.75)\n            image_change[change_idx:change_end_idx] = 1\n\n        trials.append({\n            'image_names': image_names,              # (n_frames,) object array\n            'image_change': image_change,            # (n_frames,) int8\n            'trial_outcome': outcome,\n            'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n            'running': running_speed[idx].astype(np.float32),\n            'pupil': pupil_diameter[idx].astype(np.float32),\n        })\n    return trials\n\n\ndef discretize(all_values_flat, n_levels=N_LEVELS):\n    \"\"\"Percentile-based discretization across all data.\"\"\"\n    valid = all_values_flat[~np.isnan(all_values_flat)]\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_levels + 1)\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(valid, percentiles)\n    return bin_edges\n\n\ndef apply_discretize(values, bin_edges):\n    \"\"\"Apply pre-computed bin edges. NaN -> 0 (safest discrete label).\"\"\"\n    out = np.digitize(values, bin_edges[1:-1]).astype(np.int8)\n    out[np.isnan(values)] = 0\n    return out\n\n\ndef main():\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(\n        description='Convert Allen Visual Behavior Ophys data to pickle format')\n    parser.add_argument('output', help='Output pickle file path')\n    parser.add_argument('--datadir', type=str,\n                        default='/groups/zhang/home/zhangl5/Allen_Brain',\n                        help='Allen SDK cache directory (default: /groups/zhang/home/zhangl5/Allen_Brain)')\n    mode = parser.add_mutually_exclusive_group()\n    mode.add_argument('--full', action='store_true', default=True,\n                      help='Process all mice and sessions (default)')\n    mode.add_argument('--sample', action='store_true',\n                      help='Process only 2 mice with 2 sessions each for testing')\n    args = parser.parse_args()\n\n    t0 = time.time()\n    print(\"Loading Allen cache...\")\n    bc = get_cache(args.datadir)\n    experiment_table = bc.get_ophys_experiment_table()\n\n    # Filter to VisualBehavior project only\n    vb_experiments = experiment_table[experiment_table.project_code == PROJECT_CODE]\n\n    # The datalimit dataset holds a subset of the release, listed in\n    # DATALIMIT_SUBSET.csv. The AllenSDK metadata is unmodified and still lists every\n    # released experiment, so restrict to the ids named there BEFORE anything is loaded --\n    # otherwise get_behavior_ophys_experiment() would fetch the absent ones from S3. The\n    # full dataset has no such file.\n    subset_csv = f\"{args.datadir}/DATALIMIT_SUBSET.csv\"\n    if Path(subset_csv).is_file():\n        subset_ids = pd.read_csv(subset_csv).ophys_experiment_id\n        vb_experiments = vb_experiments[vb_experiments.index.isin(subset_ids)]\n        if vb_experiments.empty:\n            sys.exit(f\"No experiments matched {subset_csv}; refusing to continue.\")\n        print(f\"Data limit: {len(vb_experiments)} experiments from {subset_csv}\")\n\n    all_mouse_ids = sorted(vb_experiments.mouse_id.unique())\n    print(f\"VisualBehavior project: {len(all_mouse_ids)} mice, \"\n          f\"{vb_experiments.ophys_session_id.nunique()} sessions, \"\n          f\"{len(vb_experiments)} experiments\")\n\n    if args.sample:\n        all_mouse_ids = all_mouse_ids[:2]\n        print(f\"  (sample mode: using first {len(all_mouse_ids)} mice)\")\n\n    # session_results: list of (mouse_id, session_data, trials)\n    session_results = []\n    subjects = []  # ordered unique mouse IDs\n    mouse_to_subj_idx = {}\n\n    global_session_count = 0\n    for mouse_id in all_mouse_ids:\n        mouse_exps = vb_experiments[vb_experiments.mouse_id == mouse_id]\n        mouse_sessions = mouse_exps.drop_duplicates(subset='ophys_session_id')[\n            ['ophys_session_id', 'session_type', 'date_of_acquisition']\n        ].sort_values(by='date_of_acquisition')\n\n        session_ids = mouse_sessions['ophys_session_id'].values\n        if args.sample:\n            session_ids = session_ids[:2]\n\n        # Register subject\n        if mouse_id not in mouse_to_subj_idx:\n            mouse_to_subj_idx[mouse_id] = len(subjects)\n            subjects.append(str(mouse_id))\n\n        print(f\"\\nMouse {mouse_id} ({mouse_exps.iloc[0]['cre_line']}): \"\n              f\"{len(session_ids)} sessions\")\n\n        for j, sid in enumerate(session_ids):\n            stype = mouse_sessions[mouse_sessions.ophys_session_id == sid].iloc[0]['session_type']\n            sess_exps = mouse_exps[mouse_exps.ophys_session_id == sid]\n            global_session_count += 1\n            print(f'  [{j+1}/{len(session_ids)}] Session {sid} ({stype}) '\n                  f'— {len(sess_exps)} planes...', end=' ', flush=True)\n\n            t1 = time.time()\n            try:\n                session_data = extract_session_data(bc, sess_exps)\n                trials = segment_trials(session_data)\n            except Exception as e:\n                print(f'FAILED: {e}')\n                continue\n            elapsed = time.time() - t1\n            print(f'{session_data[\"neural_data\"].shape[0]} neurons, '\n                  f'{len(trials)} trials ({elapsed:.1f}s)')\n\n            # Keep only small metadata; drop large full-session arrays\n            session_meta = {\n                'ophys_ts': session_data['ophys_ts'],\n                'plane_labels': session_data['plane_labels'],\n            }\n            del session_data\n            session_results.append((mouse_id, session_meta, trials))\n\n    print(f\"\\nExtracted {len(session_results)} sessions from \"\n          f\"{len(subjects)} mice\")\n\n    # ---- Compute discretization bin edges from ALL trials ----\n    print(\"\\n=== Computing discretization bin edges ===\")\n    all_running = np.concatenate([\n        np.concatenate([t['running'] for t in trials])\n        for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n    ])\n    all_pupil = np.concatenate([\n        np.concatenate([t['pupil'] for t in trials])\n        for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n    ])\n\n    run_edges = discretize(all_running, N_LEVELS)\n    pupil_edges = discretize(all_pupil, N_LEVELS)\n    print(f\"Running speed bin edges: {run_edges}\")\n    print(f\"Pupil diameter bin edges: {pupil_edges}\")\n\n    # ---- Build image code mapping (global across all sessions) ----\n    all_image_names = set()\n    for _, _, trials in session_results:\n        for t in trials:\n            all_image_names.update(t['image_names'])\n    all_image_names = sorted(all_image_names)\n    image_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(all_image_names)}\n    print(f\"\\nImage codes ({len(all_image_names)} images): {image_to_code}\")\n\n    # Trial outcome mapping\n    outcome_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(TRIAL_OUTCOMES)}\n    print(f\"Trial outcome codes: {outcome_to_code}\")\n\n    # ---- Assemble output format ----\n    print(\"\\n=== Assembling output ===\")\n    all_neural = []\n    all_input = []\n    all_output = []\n    all_region_idx = []\n    subject_idx_list = []\n\n    # Collect unique brain regions across all sessions\n    all_brain_regions = []\n    for _, session_meta, _ in session_results:\n        for lbl in session_meta['plane_labels']:\n            if lbl not in all_brain_regions:\n                all_brain_regions.append(lbl)\n\n    region_to_idx = {r: i for i, r in enumerate(all_brain_regions)}\n\n    # Compute time_bin_size from first session\n    first_ophys_ts = session_results[0][1]['ophys_ts']  # session_meta\n    time_bin_size_ms = float(np.median(np.diff(first_ophys_ts)) * 1000)\n\n    for i, (mouse_id, session_meta, trials) in enumerate(session_results):\n        if len(trials) < 2:\n\"\"\"Convert Allen Brain Observatory Visual Behavior 2P data into the decoder format.\n\nData selection:\n  * Only experiments from the change-detection (\"Visual Behavior\") task performed\n    actively by the mouse (behavior_type == 'active_behavior'); passive-viewing\n    sessions contain no trials/behavioral report and are therefore excluded.\n  * Only familiar image sets, following Garrett/Bennett et al. (\"we restricted our\n    analysis to familiar stimuli\").\n  * Only the single-plane VisualBehavior project code. All those experiments are\n    imaged at ~31 Hz, so that every session shares the same time-bin size (as\n    required by the target format). The few Multiscope (11 Hz) sessions available\n    locally (4 sessions from a single mouse) would break that requirement.\n  * Trials: the \"go\" and \"catch\" trials defined by the Allen trials table, i.e.\n    aborted and auto-rewarded trials are excluded.\n\nNeural data: detected calcium events (the paper: \"For all analysis of neural data we\nused the detected calcium events\"). We use the AllenSDK 'filtered_events' trace,\nwhich is exactly those detected events convolved with a causal half-normal kernel:\nthe raw event trace is nonzero in only ~0.3% of the 32 ms frames, which carries\nalmost no information in a single time bin, while the filtered trace keeps the event\ntimes but spreads each event over the following few frames. Each cell is then divided\nby the standard deviation of its own trace over the session, so that the per-session\nprojection learned by the decoder is not dominated by a few very active cells.\n\nAll data streams are put on one common clock: the ophys frame timestamps of the\nimaging plane (\"temporally align based on ophys timestamp\"). The stimulus table,\nrunning speed (60 Hz) and pupil (30 Hz eye-tracking camera) are resampled onto those\nframe times, so one time bin = one ophys frame (~32.3 ms).\n\"\"\"\n\nimport argparse\nimport os\nimport pickle\nimport warnings\nfrom multiprocessing import Pool\n\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\n\nwarnings.filterwarnings('ignore')\n\nDATA_DIR = '/app/data/visual-behavior-ophys-1.1.0'\nEXP_DIR = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'behavior_ophys_experiments')\nMETA = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'project_metadata', 'ophys_experiment_table.csv')\n\nOUTCOMES = ['hit', 'miss', 'false_alarm', 'correct_reject']\nNBINS = 5  # number of percentile bins for running speed and pupil diameter\n\n\ndef select_experiments():\n    meta = pd.read_csv(META)\n    have = set()\n    for f in os.listdir(EXP_DIR):\n        if f.endswith('.nwb'):\n            have.add(int(f.split('_')[-1].split('.')[0]))\n    sel = meta[meta.ophys_experiment_id.isin(have)\n               & (meta.behavior_type == 'active_behavior')\n               & (meta.experience_level == 'Familiar')\n               & (meta.project_code == 'VisualBehavior')].copy()\n    sel = sel.sort_values('ophys_experiment_id').reset_index(drop=True)\n    return sel\n\n\ndef process_experiment(args):\n    eid, signal = args\n    from allensdk.brain_observatory.behavior.behavior_ophys_experiment import (\n        BehaviorOphysExperiment)\n    exp = BehaviorOphysExperiment.from_nwb_path(\n        os.path.join(EXP_DIR, f'behavior_ophys_experiment_{eid}.nwb'))\n\n    ts = np.asarray(exp.ophys_timestamps, dtype=np.float64)\n    nframes = len(ts)\n\n    # ---- neural: detected calcium events on the ophys frame clock -------------\n    ev_table = exp.events\n    cell_ids = list(ev_table.index.values)\n    neural = np.stack([np.asarray(x, dtype=np.float32)\n                       for x in ev_table[signal].values])  # (ncells, nframes)\n    assert neural.shape[1] == nframes\n    # Scale each cell by the standard deviation of its trace over the whole session.\n    # Event magnitudes are in units of dF/F and vary by orders of magnitude between\n    # cells (and between cre lines / imaging depths); without this, the per-session\n    # projection of the decoder is dominated by a few high-variance cells.\n    sd = neural.std(axis=1, keepdims=True)\n    sd[sd == 0] = 1.0\n    neural = neural / sd\n\n    # ---- stimulus: image identity and image change ----------------------------\n    sp = exp.stimulus_presentations\n    flashes = sp[(sp.active == True) & (sp.omitted == False)]  # noqa: E712\n    image_names = sorted(flashes.image_name.unique())\n    image_id = np.zeros(nframes, dtype=np.int64)   # 0 == gray screen / omitted\n    change = np.zeros(nframes, dtype=np.int64)\n    starts = np.searchsorted(ts, flashes.start_time.values, side='left')\n    stops = np.searchsorted(ts, flashes.end_time.values, side='left')\n    idx = np.array([image_names.index(n) + 1 for n in flashes.image_name.values])\n    is_change = flashes.is_change.values.astype(bool)\n    for a, b, i, c in zip(starts, stops, idx, is_change):\n        image_id[a:b] = i\n        if c:\n            change[a:b] = 1\n\n    # ---- running speed --------------------------------------------------------\n    rs = exp.running_speed\n    running = np.interp(ts, rs.timestamps.values, rs.speed.values)\n\n    # ---- pupil diameter -------------------------------------------------------\n    et = exp.eye_tracking\n    if len(et) == 0:\n        return dict(eid=eid, skip='no eye tracking')\n    area = et.pupil_area.values.astype(np.float64).copy()\n    area[et.likely_blink.values.astype(bool)] = np.nan  # blinks are not measurements\n    diam = 2.0 * np.sqrt(area / np.pi)\n    good = np.isfinite(diam)\n    if good.sum() < 100:\n        return dict(eid=eid, skip='no valid pupil samples')\n    et_ts = et.timestamps.values.astype(np.float64)\n    # interpolate the (blink) gaps, on the eye-tracking clock, then onto ophys frames\n    pupil = np.interp(ts, et_ts[good], diam[good])\n    # frames whose nearest valid pupil sample is far away are flagged as missing\n    nearest = np.abs(ts - et_ts[good][np.clip(\n        np.searchsorted(et_ts[good], ts), 0, good.sum() - 1)])\n    nearest_prev = np.abs(ts - et_ts[good][np.clip(\n        np.searchsorted(et_ts[good], ts) - 1, 0, good.sum() - 1)])\n    gap = np.minimum(nearest, nearest_prev)\n    pupil_valid = gap < 0.5  # within half a second of a real measurement\n\n    # ---- trials ---------------------------------------------------------------\n    tr = exp.trials\n    gc = tr[(tr.go | tr.catch)]\n    trials = []\n    for tid, row in gc.iterrows():\n        a = int(np.searchsorted(ts, row.start_time, side='left'))\n        b = int(np.searchsorted(ts, row.stop_time, side='left'))\n        if b - a < 2:\n            continue\n        if np.mean(pupil_valid[a:b]) < 0.5:\n            continue  # pupil not tracked for most of this trial (blink / lost eye)\n        oc = [o for o in OUTCOMES if bool(row[o])]\n        if len(oc) != 1:\n            continue\n        trials.append(dict(\n            neural=neural[:, a:b].copy(),\n            image_id=image_id[a:b].copy(),\n            change=change[a:b].copy(),\n            running=running[a:b].copy(),\n            pupil=pupil[a:b].copy(),\n            outcome=OUTCOMES.index(oc[0]),\n            trial_id=int(tid),\n            change_time=float(row.change_time),\n            start_time=float(row.start_time),\n            stop_time=float(row.stop_time),\n        ))\n\n    md = exp.metadata\n    return dict(\n        eid=eid, skip=None, trials=trials, cell_ids=cell_ids,\n        image_names=image_names, dt=float(np.median(np.diff(ts))),\n        mouse_id=str(md['mouse_id']), structure=str(md['targeted_structure']),\n        cre_line=str(md['cre_line']), session_type=str(md['session_type']),\n        ophys_session_id=int(md['ophys_session_id']),\n        imaging_depth=int(md['imaging_depth']),\n        frame_rate=float(md['ophys_frame_rate']),\n        ntrials_total=int(len(gc)),\n    )\n\n\ndef main():\n    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()\n    ap.add_argument('--out', default='/app/converted_data.pkl')\n    ap.add_argument('--signal', default='filtered_events',\n                    choices=['events', 'filtered_events'])\n    ap.add_argument('--nsessions', type=int, default=0,\n                    help='limit number of sessions (0 = all), for pilot runs')\n    ap.add_argument('--workers', type=int, default=16)\n    ap.add_argument('--binning', default='session', choices=['session', 'global'])\n    args = ap.parse_args()\n\n    sel = select_experiments()\n    if args.nsessions:\n        sel = sel.iloc[:args.nsessions]\n    eids = [int(x) for x in sel.ophys_experiment_id.values]\n    print(f'processing {len(eids)} experiments with signal={args.signal}')\n\n    with Pool(args.workers) as pool:\n        results = pool.map(process_experiment, [(e, args.signal) for e in eids])\n\n    results = [r for r in results if r.get('skip') is None and len(r['trials']) >= 2]\n    print(f'{len(results)} sessions kept')\n\n    # ---- percentile bins for running speed and pupil diameter -----------------\n    # Pupil size is measured in camera pixels and its absolute value differs by up\n    # to ~3x between sessions/mice (eye size, camera position), and mice also differ\n    # strongly in how much they run. The decoder has a separate projection per\n    # session, so only within-session variation can be decoded. Bin edges are\n    # therefore computed per session (equal-occupancy quintiles within a session),\n    # unless --binning global is requested.\n    qs = np.linspace(0, 100, NBINS + 1)[1:-1]\n    if args.binning == 'global':\n        run_all = np.concatenate([t['running'] for r in results for t in r['trials']])\n        pup_all = np.concatenate([t['pupil'] for r in results for t in r['trials']])\n        edges = {r['eid']: (np.percentile(run_all, qs), np.percentile(pup_all, qs))\n                 for r in results}\n    else:\n        edges = {}\n        for r in results:\n
